**Q:** tell me about $mx, the semiconductor company

**A:**

→ **$MX $6.68** as of May 27, 2026 — market cap **~$246M**, trading at a deep discount to book value with a strong cash position (~$104M) relative to its enterprise value

→ **Business Model:** South Korean-based analog/mixed-signal chipmaker currently executing a "333" turnaround strategy to pivot into a pure-play power semiconductor company, shedding legacy display driver IC (DDIC) operations to focus on higher-margin power products

→ **Key Segments:** Power Analog Solutions (PAS) and Power IC (PIC) businesses, producing MOSFETs, IGBTs, and PMICs for automotive, industrial, and AI data center power infrastructure — recently launched 8th-gen MOSFETs targeting smartphone battery efficiency and server power systems

→ **Competitive Landscape:** Caught in a "pinsir" movement — squeezed at the high end by giants like **Infineon** and at the low end by state-backed Chinese firms flooding the market with cheap alternatives; revenue is highly concentrated, with ~75% coming from just 10 customers

→ **Catalyst/Outlook:** Strategic partnership with **Hyundai Mobis** for EV traction inverter IGBTs is a key long-term play; management is banking on new-generation products (AI servers, EV, solar) to drive margin expansion, though profitability remains elusive with negative EPS and ongoing pricing in-the-red EBIT margins

**A:**

Success isn't about finding a daddy to bail you out of an $8k hole; it’s about having a system that doesn't rely on martingaling your way back to green every time you tilt. If you want to stop fumbling, stop chasing the lotto tickets and actually take the profits when they’re sitting right in front of you instead of watching them evaporate. Abe’s edge isn't magic, it’s just not letting a bad day turn into a career-ender.

## 2026-05-29 19:52:18 UTC

**Asker:** Wock (`sleep04025`) in #💬-stonks-yapping-💬

**Q:** what are they talking about in regards to imbalances

**A:**

→ An order imbalance occurs when there is a significant disparity between buy and sell orders at the market close, typically driven by institutional rebalancing, index fund adjustments, or large-scale hedging.

→ Exchanges publish these imbalances leading into the close; traders monitor them to gauge the direction of the final print and anticipate potential volatility.

→ Large imbalances often trigger rapid price swings in the final minutes of the session as market makers and high-frequency algorithms scramble to provide liquidity to fill the gap.

→ When the room mentions "imbalances" during a move, they are referencing the "MOC" (Market on Close) data, which dictates the final price action and can override existing technical trends.

## 2026-05-29 19:52:51 UTC

**Asker:** Wock (`sleep04025`) in #💬-stonks-yapping-💬

**Q:** [MESSAGE BEING REPLIED TO — from omniwiz — user_id 1422761344322502807]
"→ An order imbalance occurs when there is a significant disparity between buy and sell orders at the market close, typically driven by institutional rebalancing, index fund adjustments, or large-scale hedging.

→ Exchanges publish these imbalances leading into the close; traders monitor them to gauge the direction of the final print and anticipate potential volatility.

→ Large imbalances often trigger rapid price swings in the final minutes of the session as market makers and high-frequency algorithms scramble to provide liquidity to fill the gap.

→ When the room mentions "imbalances" during a move, they are referencing the "MOC" (Market on Close) data, which dictates the final price action and can override existing technical trends."

[Wock's message to you]
explain it simpler to me, why did the charts move so much for Google and nvda

**A:**

→ Think of the "Market on Close" (MOC) as a massive, mandatory rebalancing event for institutional funds. These funds are required to hold stocks in exact proportion to their weight in major indices like the S&P 500 or Nasdaq.

→ Because $NVDA and $GOOG are two of the largest components in those indices, any shift in index weightings forces these funds to buy or sell millions of shares of these specific stocks simultaneously to stay compliant.

→ That massive, forced volume hits the tape right at 4:00 PM ET. Market makers have to facilitate those trades, which causes the price to swing violently in the final minutes, regardless of what the chart looked like earlier in the day.

## 2026-05-29 19:53:16 UTC

**Asker:** Wock (`sleep04025`) in #💬-stonks-yapping-💬

**Q:** [MESSAGE BEING REPLIED TO — from omniwiz — user_id 1422761344322502807]
"→ Think of the "Market on Close" (MOC) as a massive, mandatory rebalancing event for institutional funds. These funds are required to hold stocks in exact proportion to their weight in major indices like the S&P 500 or Nasdaq.

→ Because $NVDA and $GOOG are two of the largest components in those indices, any shift in index weightings forces these funds to buy or sell millions of shares of these specific stocks simultaneously to stay compliant.

→ That massive, forced volume hits the tape right at 4:00 PM ET. Market makers have to facilitate those trades, which causes the price to swing violently in the final minutes, regardless of what the chart looked like earlier in the day."

[Wock's message to you]
so which stocks were then added to correct the imbalance?

**A:**

→ The "imbalance" isn't a problem being fixed by adding stocks; it is the net buy or sell pressure created when institutional funds are forced to adjust their holdings to match new index weightings at the market close.

→ Index providers like MSCI do periodically add or remove stocks during "reconstitution," but that is a separate process from the daily MOC rebalancing flow that caused the volatility in $NVDA and $GOOG today.

→ The price swings you saw were not because stocks were "added" to correct an imbalance, but because those specific tickers have such massive weightings that even minor index adjustments force billions in passive buying or selling to hit the tape at 4:00 PM ET.

→ In short: the imbalance is the *result* of the rebalancing, not the reason for it. Market makers have to facilitate that massive, mandatory volume, which forces the price to move regardless of the earlier chart setup.

## 2026-05-29 19:56:11 UTC

**Asker:** Wock (`sleep04025`) in #💬-stonks-yapping-💬

**Q:** how do we know when imbalances take place

**A:**

→ Imbalances occur when buy and sell orders are significantly mismatched, forcing price adjustments to find liquidity; they are most visible during auction periods like the market open and close.

→ Exchanges disseminate "Market on Close" (MOC) imbalance data starting at **3:50 PM ET**, showing the net buy or sell pressure for the closing auction; this is the primary signal for institutional rebalancing.

→ Index rebalancing (e.g., S&P 500 quarterly reconstitution) is a predictable systematic event where passive funds must force-trade constituents to match index weights, creating massive, scheduled liquidity events.

→ Real-time identification requires monitoring order book depth—specifically the ratio of resting bids vs. asks at the top levels—or using order flow tools to track aggressive market orders hitting the tape-reading against resting limit liquidity.

## 2026-05-29 19:56:32 UTC

**Asker:** Wock (`sleep04025`) in #💬-stonks-yapping-💬

**Q:** [MESSAGE BEING REPLIED TO — from omniwiz — user_id 1422761344322502807]
"→ Imbalances occur when buy and sell orders are significantly mismatched, forcing price adjustments to find liquidity; they are most visible during auction periods like the market open and close.

→ Exchanges disseminate "Market on Close" (MOC) imbalance data starting at **3:50 PM ET**, showing the net buy or sell pressure for the closing auction; this is the primary signal for institutional rebalancing.

→ Index rebalancing (e.g., S&P 500 quarterly reconstitution) is a predictable systematic event where passive funds must force-trade constituents to match index weights, creating massive, scheduled liquidity events.

→ Real-time identification requires monitoring order book depth—specifically the ratio of resting bids vs. asks at the top levels—or using order flow tools to track aggressive market orders hitting the tape-reading against resting limit liquidity."

[Wock's message to you]
Stop being retarded is this like a monthly or weekly thing or what

**A:**

→ MOC imbalances are a **daily** event — every single trading day at the close, exchanges publish the net buy/sell pressure for the auction.

→ Index rebalancing is **quarterly** — passive funds force-trade to match index weights, creating the massive, scheduled liquidity events you see on those specific dates.

→ Don't confuse the two — daily imbalances are about intraday liquidity and closing price discovery; quarterly rebalancing is about institutional portfolio maintenance.
